Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n    10→\n    11→2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n    12→\n    13→3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **paper** \"A flexible hippocampal population code for experience relative to reward\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n    27→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    28→- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n    29→- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n    30→- **Documentation** on nwb file format is at https://nwb-schema.readthedocs.io/en/latest/\n    31→\n    32→## Decoder Task\n    33→\n    34→Temporally align based on start of the trial. \n    35→\n    36→### Decoder Inputs:\n    37→- Time from start of trial in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n    38→- Environment type (binary, ENV1 vs ENV2, per trial)\n    39→- Trial number (continuous, per trial)\n    40→- Previous trial outcome (binary, omitted = 0, rewarded = 1, per trial)\n    41→\n    42→### Decoder Outputs\n    43→- Distance to any location in the reward zone, time-varying. Discretization bins:\n    44→  - 0:  < -50 cm\n    45→  - 1: -50 to -10 cm\n    46→  - 2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n    47→  - 3: 0 cm\n    48→  - 4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n    49→  - 5: +10 to +50 cm\n    50→  - 6: > +50 cm\n    51→- Absolute position in corridor, discretized into 5 equal-sized bins, time-varying\n    52→- Speed, time-varying. Discretized into bins:\n    53→  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n    54→  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n    55→  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n    56→  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n    57→  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n    58→- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n    59→- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n    60→- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n    61→\n    62→## Target Data Format\n    63→\n    64→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    65→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    66→\n    67→```python\n    68→data = {\n    69→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    70→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    71→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    72→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    73→            ...\n    74→        ],\n    75→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    76→            ...\n    77→        ],\n    78→        ...\n    79→    ],\n    80→\n    81→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    82→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    83→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    84→            ...\n    85→        ],\n    86→        ...\n    87→    ],\n    88→\n    89→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    90→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    91→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    92→            ...\n    93→        ],\n    94→        ...\n    95→    ],\n    96→\n    97→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    98→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    99→\n   100→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n   101→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n   102→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n   103→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n   104→\n   105→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n   106→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n   107→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n   108→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n   109→        ...\n   110→    ],\n   111→\n   112→    'metadata': {\n   113→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   114→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   115→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   116→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   117→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   118→        ...\n   119→    }\n   120→}\n   121→```\n   122→\n   123→### Key Format Requirements\n   124→\n   125→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   126→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   127→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   128→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   129→\n   130→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   131→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   132→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   133→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   134→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   135→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   136→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   137→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   138→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   139→\n   140→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   141→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   142→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   143→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   144→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   145→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   146→\n   147→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   148→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   149→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   150→\n   151→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   152→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   153→\n   154→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   155→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   156→\n   157→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   158→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   159→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   160→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   161→\n   162→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   163→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   164→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   165→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   166→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   167→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   168→\n   169→## Python environment\n   170→\n   171→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   172→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   173→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   174→\n   175→## Decoder Reference\n   176→\n   177→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   178→- Optional arguments:\n   179→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   180→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   181→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   182→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   183→\n   184→### Purpose\n   185→\n   186→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   187→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   188→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   189→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   190→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   191→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   192→\n   193→### Consistency requirements\n   194→\n   195→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   196→- Loading of data\n   197→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   198→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   199→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   200→\n   201→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   202→\n   203→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   204→\n   205→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   206→\n   207→Carefully verify your work after every step. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   210→\n   211→---\n   212→\n   213→## Conversion Workflow\n   214→\n   215→**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Experimental and behavioral variables\n   313→  - Data processing\n   314→  - Data curation\n   315→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   316→\n   317→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   318→\n   319→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   320→\n   321→---\n   322→\n   323→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   324→\n   325→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   326→\n   327→**Actions**:\n   328→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   329→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   330→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   331→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   332→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   333→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   334→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   335→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   336→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   337→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   338→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   339→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   340→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   341→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   342→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   343→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   344→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   345→\n   346→**Done when**:\n   347→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   348→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   349→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   350→\n   351→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   352→\n   353→---\n   354→\n   355→### Step 6: Script Development\n   356→\n   357→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   358→\n   359→**Actions**:\n   360→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   361→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   362→- Include options:\n   363→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   364→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   365→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   366→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   367→- Write efficient code:\n   368→  - Vectorize loops\n   369→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   370→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   371→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   372→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   373→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   374→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   375→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   376→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   377→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   378→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   379→\n   380→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   381→\n   382→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   383→\n   384→---\n   385→\n   386→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   387→\n   388→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   389→\n   390→**Actions**:\n   391→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   392→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   393→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   394→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   395→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   396→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   397→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   398→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   399→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   400→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   401→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   402→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   403→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   404→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   405→\n   406→**Done when**: \n   407→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   408→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   409→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   410→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   411→\n   412→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   413→\n   414→---\n   415→\n   416→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   417→\n   418→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   419→\n   420→**Actions**:\n   421→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   422→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   423→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   424→\n   425→**Investigate any issues**:\n   426→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   427→\n   428→**Done when**: \n   429→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   430→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   431→- Accuracies are above chance\n   432→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   433→\n   434→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   435→\n   436→---\n   437→\n   438→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   439→\n   440→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   441→\n   442→**Actions**:\n   443→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   444→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   445→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   446→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   447→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   448→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   449→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   450→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   451→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   452→\n   453→**Done when**:\n   454→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   455→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   456→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   457→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   458→\n   459→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   460→\n   461→---\n   462→\n   463→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   464→\n   465→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   466→\n   467→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   468→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   469→\n   470→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   471→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   472→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   473→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   474→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   475→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   476→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   477→\n   478→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   479→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   480→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   481→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   482→- Fix any mismatches. \n   483→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   484→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   485→\n   486→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   487→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   488→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   489→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   490→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   491→\n   492→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   493→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   494→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   495→\n   496→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   497→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   498→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   499→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   500→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   501→\n   502→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   503→\n   504→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   505→\n   506→---\n   507→\n   508→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   509→\n   510→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   511→\n   512→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   513→\n   514→**Actions**:\n   515→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   516→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   517→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   518→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   519→\n   520→**Done when**: \n   521→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   522→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   523→\n   524→---\n   525→\n   526→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   527→\n   528→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   529→\n   530→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   531→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   532→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   533→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   534→\n   535→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   536→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   537→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   538→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   539→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   540→\n   541→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   542→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   543→\n   544→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   545→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   546→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   547→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   548→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   549→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n   550→\n   551→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   552→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   553→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   554→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   555→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   556→\n   557→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   558→\n   559→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   560→\n   561→---\n   562→\n   563→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   564→\n   565→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   566→\n   567→**Actions**:\n   568→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   569→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   570→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   571→  - Validation results and tables\n   572→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   573→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   574→  - Dataset description\n   575→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   576→  - Output format specification\n   577→  - Key statistics\n   578→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   579→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   580→\n   581→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   582→\n   583→---\n   584→\n   585→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   586→\n   587→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. data\n   839→- **Trial alignment**: Align trials to meaningful events (stimulus onset, movement, etc.)\n   840→- **Time windows**: Choose appropriate pre/post-event windows\n   841→- **Neuron curation**: Choose filtering criteria for the quality of each neuron's recording based on reference texts\n   842→- **Trial curation**: Choose quality filtering criteria for trials based on reference texts or bad or missing data\n   843→\n   844→### Computational Efficiency\n   845→- Be mindful of memory usage with large datasets\n   846→- Use appropriate data types (float32 vs. float64)\n   847→- Consider lazy loading for very large datasets\n   848→- Implement efficient array operations (vectorization)\n   849→\n   850→## Success Criteria\n   851→\n   852→A successful conversion should:\n   853→1. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"A flexible hippocampal population code for experience relative to reward\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n- **Documentation** on nwb file format is at https://nwb-schema.readthedocs.io/en/latest/\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on start of the trial. \n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time from start of trial in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n- Environment type (binary, ENV1 vs ENV2, per trial)\n- Trial number (continuous, per trial)\n- Previous trial outcome (binary, omitted = 0, rewarded = 1, per trial)\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Distance to any location in the reward zone, time-varying. Discretization bins:\n  - 0:  < -50 cm\n  - 1: -50 to -10 cm\n  - 2: -10 cm to < 0 cm\n  - 3: 0 cm\n  - 4: >0 cm to +10 cm\n  - 5: +10 to +50 cm\n  - 6: > +50 cm\n- Absolute position in corridor, discretized into 5 equal-sized bins, time-varying\n- Speed, time-varying. Discretized into bins:\n  - 0: < 2 cm/s\n  - 1: 2-10 cm/s\n  - 2: 10-20 cm/s\n  - 3: 20-40 cm/s\n  - 4: > 40 cm/s\n- Lick, time-varying. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n- Reward zone location, per-trial. 0 = A, 1 = B, 2 = C\n- Reward outcome, per-trial. 0 = no, 1 = yes\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n   283→\n   284→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n   285→\n   286→i. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n   287→\n   288→ii.\n   289→```python\n   290→if trial == 0:\n   291→    input_curr[3,:] = 0\n   292→else:\n   293→    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n   294→    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n   295→    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n   296→```\n   297→\n   298→iii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n   299→\n   300→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n   301→\n   302→i. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n   303→\n   304→ii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n   305→- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n   306→```python\n   307→behavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \n   308→info['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\n   309→position = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\n   310→for trial in range(ntrials):\n   311→    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   312→    pos_trial = position[idx]\n   313→    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n   314→    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n   315→        continue\n   316→    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n   317→```\n   318→The function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n   319→```python\n   320→reward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n   321→                    'B': [200, 250],\n   322→                    'C': [320, 370]}\n   323→...\n   324→sigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\n   325→zone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\n   326→dist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n   327→                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\n   328→dist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\n   329→log_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n   330→```\n   331→It also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n   332→```python\n   333→pstay = .9\n   334→log_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\n   335→np.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n   336→```\n   337→\n   338→iii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n   339→\n   340→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n   341→\n   342→i. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.\n   343→\n   344→ii.\n   345→```python\n   346→def compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n   347→    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n   348→    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n   349→    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n   350→    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n   351→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n   352→    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n   353→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n   354→    return distance_to_reward_zone\n   355→```\n   356→\n   357→iii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`.\n   358→\n   359→## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n   360→\n   361→i. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n   362→\n   363→ii.\n   364→```python\n   365→distance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n   366→...\n   367→output_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n   368→```\n   369→\n   370→iii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n   371→\n   372→## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n   373→\n   374→i. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n   375→\n   376→ii.\n   377→```python\n   378→idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   379→spike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\n   380→position_curr = position[idx]\n   381→```\n   382→\n   383→iii. Both neural and behavioral data use the same time indexing (verified by timestamp assertions earlier in the code), so indexing by the same `idx` array ensures alignment.\n   384→\n   385→## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Absolute position* derived from?\n   386→\n   387→i. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   515→\n   516→i. Several cases are handled:\n   517→- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If the number of neural time bins differs from behavior timestamps, data is cropped to the minimum of the two with a warning printed.\n   518→- **Short trials**: Trials with fewer than 50 timepoints (`min_ntimepoints`) are skipped with a warning.\n   519→- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active (all zeros) get NaN positions, and the Viterbi algorithm handles these via uniform emission (assignment driven by neighboring trials' transitions).\n   520→- **Reward timestamp alignment**: An assertion verifies reward times align within half a time bin of behavior timestamps.\n   521→\n   522→ii.\n   523→```python\n   524→if T != len(timestamps):\n   525→    print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n   526→    T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n   527→    spike_data = spike_data[:T]\n   528→    timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n   529→...\n   530→if idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n   531→    print(f\"Skipping trial {trial}...\")\n   532→    istrial[trial] = False\n   533→    continue\n   534→```\n   535→\n   536→iii. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* derived from? \n\ni. This input is derived from the `timestamps` variable that is available for every behavior time series. These were all the same, so I used the `trial_number` time series. \n\nii. \n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series\ntimestamps = behavior_data['trial number'].timestamps[:] \nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    timestamps_curr = timestamps[idx]\n    ...\n    input_curr[0] = timestamps_curr - timestamps_curr[0]\n```\n\niii. Behavior timestamps were consistent with the constant neural data rate, many choices would have resulted in the same result. \n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds*?\n\ni. The initial timestamp for the trial is subtracted. \n\nii. \n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series\ntimestamps = behavior_data['trial number'].timestamps[:] \nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    timestamps_curr = timestamps[idx]\n    ...\n    input_curr[0] = timestamps_curr - timestamps_curr[0]\n```\n\niii. Common sense\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of trial in seconds* aligned with the neural data? \n\ni. Neural and behavioral data is already temporally aligned in the stored data, nothing needed to be done. A check was made that the number of time points was the same in the neural and behavioral data, and cropped to the shortest if they did not match\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. Data rates for the neural and behavior data were the same, and all offsets were 0. \n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment type* derived from? \n\ni. `environment` behavior time series\n\nii.\n```python\nvr_environment = behavior_data['environment'].data[:][:T] \nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    ...\n    vr_environment_curr = vr_environment[idx].astype(int)\n    input_curr[1] = vr_environment_curr\n```\n\niii. From inspection, this environment variable is constant in the trial and can only be 0 or 1, which matches the ENV1, ENV2 variable description in the paper. \n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment type*?\n\ni. None\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. N/A\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Trial number* derived from?\n\ni. Trial number is the within-session trial index, derived from the loop counter over trials. The trial boundaries themselves come from the `trial_start` and `teleport` behavior time series.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    ...\n    input_curr[2] = trial\n```\n\niii. The trial number is a sequential index (0, 1, 2, ...) within a session. The stored `trial number` variable in the NWB file was not used because it did not agree with the `trial_start` variable.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number*?\n\ni. No processing beyond assigning the loop index. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\ninput_curr[2] = trial\n```\n\niii. The trial number is simply the sequential index of the trial within the session.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Previous trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `Reward` behavior time series. Reward event timestamps are matched to behavior timestamps, and a binary `isreward` array marks timepoints where rewards occurred.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_amounts = behavior_data['Reward'].data[:][:T]\nreward_times = behavior_data['Reward'].timestamps[:]\nreward_time_indices = np.searchsorted(timestamps, reward_times)\nisreward = np.zeros(T)\nisreward[reward_time_indices] = 1\n```\n\niii. The `Reward` time series has its own timestamps (not aligned to the behavior sampling rate), so `searchsorted` is used to find the nearest timepoint. An assertion checks that the alignment error is within half a time bin.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Previous trial outcome*?\n\ni. For each trial, check whether any reward event occurred in the *previous* trial's time range. For the first trial, set to 0. The value is constant across all timepoints within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nif trial == 0:\n    input_curr[3,:] = 0\nelse:\n    prev_reward_idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial-1], trial_end_idx[trial-1], dtype=int)\n    isprevreward = np.any(isreward[prev_reward_idx])\n    input_curr[3,:] = int(isprevreward)\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify previous trial outcome as binary (omitted=0, rewarded=1). The code checks if any reward event fell within the previous trial's time boundaries.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Distance to reward zone* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series and the reward zone location for the current trial. The reward zone boundaries come from the `reward_zone` behavior time series. This variable is non-zero when the mouse is in the reward zone. I combine this with the position of the mouse when this is non-zero to figure out the closest reward zone. To allow for some noise (of unknown source) and unrewarded tasks, I use the Viterbi algorithm to assign zones so that the rewarded positions match the known reward zones and it is uncommon to switch reward zone. \n\nii. Assigning reward zone location for each trial: \n- Before converting, the `survey()` function was used to try to understand all the data values, including whatever is going on with the `reward_zone` variable. It first computes the position of the min and max position of the mouse when `reward_zone` is non-zero:\n```python\nbehavior_data = nwbfile.processing['behavior']['BehavioralTimeSeries'].time_series       \ninfo['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:]\nfor trial in range(ntrials):\n    idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n    pos_trial = position[idx]\n    reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n    if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n        continue\n    info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n```\nThe function `segment_reward_positions()` uses the Viterbi algorithm to assign reward zone labels so that the initial rewarded positions are close to the corresponding rewarded zone:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [80, 130],\n                    'B': [200, 250],\n                    'C': [320, 370]}\n...\nsigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\nzone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\ndist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                    startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\ndist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\nlog_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n```\nIt also encourages the reward zone label to not change often:\n```python\npstay = .9\nlog_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\nnp.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n```\n\niii. The paper defines reward zones A, B, C at specific position ranges. These same ranges could also be found, with some translation, in the code (required mapping X -> A, etc.). I plotted the position of the mouse when `reward_zone` was positive (note that the value was between 0 and 6 I think) to determine that the Viterbi approach would likely work, and that the start of the rewarded position was the least noisy variable to use. The class fractions were approximately 1/3 after this, so it seemed correct. \n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Distance to reward zone*?\n\ni. For each timepoint, compute the signed distance from the animal's position to the nearest edge of the reward zone. Distance is 0 when inside the zone, negative when before the zone, and positive when past it.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0]\n    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1]\n    return distance_to_reward_zone\n```\n\niii. This matches the paper's concept of distance relative to the reward zone. The reward zone boundaries are from `reward_zone_dict`, inspired by the reference code `Sosa_et_al_2024/src/reward_relative/behavior.py`.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The continuous distance is discretized into 7 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, inf]`.\n\nii.\n```python\ndistance_to_reward_zone_bins = [-np.inf, -50, -10, 0, 1e-6, 10, 50, np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[0] = np.digitize(distance_to_reward_zone, distance_to_reward_zone_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. The bin edges match the instructions. The `1e-6` boundary separates exactly 0 (in the zone boundary) from slightly positive. The `-1` corrects `np.digitize` to be 0-indexed.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Distance to reward zone* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The position data and neural data share the same time indices within each trial, so no additional alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\nidx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\nspike_data_curr = spike_data[idx]\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. Both neural and behavioral data use the same time indexing (verified by timestamp assertions earlier in the code), so indexing by the same `idx` array ensures alignment.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Absolute position* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `position` behavior time series.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition = behavior_data['position'].data[:][:T]\nassert np.allclose(behavior_data['position'].timestamps[:][:T], timestamps), \"Timestamps for position do not match trial number timestamps\"\n...\nposition_curr = position[idx]\n```\n\niii. The `position` variable directly records the animal's position in the VR corridor in cm.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Absolute position*?\n\ni. The raw position values are used directly.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Absolute position* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Discretized into 5 bins using `np.digitize` with bin edges `[-inf, 50, 150, 250, 350, inf]`, corresponding to 100 cm wide bins spanning the corridor. The position ranged from -50 to 450. \n\nii.\n```python\nposition_bins = [-np.inf,50,150,250,350,np.inf]\n...\noutput_curr[1] = np.digitize(position_curr, position_bins) - 1\n```\n\niii. Same indexing as neural: `position_curr = position[idx]`\n\niii. Verified by timestamp assertions.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Lick* derived from?\n\ni. The alignment error is checked to be within half a time bin. `np.any` is used because a trial either was rewarded or not — the specific timepoint doesn't matter for the per-trial binary output.\n\n## 12. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several cases are handled:\n- **Neural/behavior length mismatch**: If the number of neural time bins differs from behavior timestamps, data is cropped to the minimum of the two with a warning printed.\n- **Short trials**: Trials with fewer than 50 timepoints (`min_ntimepoints`) are skipped with a warning.\n- **Missing reward zone data**: Trials where `reward_zone` is never active (all zeros) get NaN positions, and the Viterbi algorithm handles these via uniform emission (assignment driven by neighboring trials' transitions).\n- **Reward timestamp alignment**: An assertion verifies reward times align within half a time bin of behavior timestamps.\n\nii.\n```python\nif T != len(timestamps):\n    print(f\"Warning: number of neural time bins ({T}) does not match number of behavior timestamps ({len(timestamps)}), cropping to the minimum of the two\")\n    T = min(T, len(timestamps))\n    spike_data = spike_data[:T]\n    timestamps = timestamps[:T]\n...\nif idx.sum() < min_ntimepoints:\n    print(f\"Skipping trial {trial}...\")\n    istrial[trial] = False\n    continue\n```\n\niii. These are defensive checks found during data exploration. The neural/behavior mismatch occurs in a small number of sessions.\n\n## 13-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming steps are:\n1. **Loading NWB files** (`pynwb.NWBHDF5IO` and reading large arrays) — I/O bound\n2. **The survey step** — loads every NWB file to gather statistics and reward zone positions\n3. **Full conversion** — loads every NWB file again and extracts all trial data\n4. **Saving the .pkl file**\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The NWB files are large (contain full neural recordings). Conversion is otherwise quite fast\n\n## 13-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop in `convert_session_data` (lines 267-332) iterates over each trial sequentially. Some operations like `np.digitize` and `compute_distance_to_reward_zone` could theoretically be applied to the full session arrays before splitting into trials, but the variable trial lengths make this awkward. The Viterbi inner loop (lines 503-507) iterates over states, which could be vectorized with matrix operations.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The per-trial loop is the natural structure given variable-length trials. Vectorizing would require padding or masking.\n\n## 13-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The survey step and the conversion step both load every NWB file and read the same data arrays (neural, behavior). The survey reads neural data to count neurons and check rates, then the conversion reads it again. The `reward_zone_positions` are computed in the survey and then used in the conversion, but other data (position, speed, lick, etc.) is read twice. \n\nii. N/A\n\niii. I don't think these could be run at the same time, as it would require too much RAM. The Viterbi output created during surveying is required to process the data. It was convenient to run the survey before deciding how to handle the conversion of the data. \n\n## 13-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. behavior_data['reward_zone'].data[:]\n   125→        info['unique_reward_zone'] = np.unique(reward_zone[istrial])\n   126→        scanning = behavior_data['scanning'].data[:]\n   127→        info['unique_scanning'] = np.unique(scanning[istrial])\n   128→        speed = behavior_data['speed'].data[:]\n   129→        info['speed_range'] = (speed[istrial].min(), speed[istrial].max())\n   130→        teleport = behavior_data['teleport'].data[:]\n   131→        info['unique_teleport'] = np.unique(teleport[istrial])\n   132→        \n   133→        # trial number and trial start do not agree with each other\n   134→        # use trial_start and teleport to find starts and ends of trials\n   135→        trial_start_idx, trial_end_idx = get_trial_starts_ends(behavior_data['trial_start'].data[:], teleport)\n   136→        trial_length = trial_end_idx - trial_start_idx\n   137→        info['trial_length_range'] = (trial_length.min(), trial_length.max())\n   138→\n   139→        ntrials = len(trial_start_idx)\n   140→        info['ntrials'] = ntrials\n   141→        info['reward_zone_positions'] = np.full((ntrials, 2), np.nan)\n   142→        for trial in range(ntrials):\n   143→            idx = np.arange(trial_start_idx[trial], trial_end_idx[trial], dtype=int)\n   144→            pos_trial = position[idx]\n   145→            reward_zone_trial = reward_zone[idx] > 0\n   146→            if not np.any(reward_zone_trial):\n   147→                continue\n   148→            info['reward_zone_positions'][trial] = [np.min(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial]), np.max(pos_trial[reward_zone_trial])]\n   149→                    \n   150→    return info\n   151→    \n   152→def get_bin_centers(bin_edges,data):\n   153→    # replace infinity with the max/min of the data for plotting purposes\n   154→    bin_edges_plot = np.array(bin_edges.copy())\n   155→    if np.isinf(bin_edges_plot[0]):\n   156→        bin_edges_plot[0] = data.min()\n   157→        if bin_edges_plot[0] > bin_edges_plot[1]:\n   158→            bin_edges_plot[0] = 2*bin_edges_plot[1] - bin_edges_plot[2] # extrapolate to get a lower value for the first bin edge\n   159→    if np.isinf(bin_edges_plot[-1]):\n   160→        bin_edges_plot[-1] = data.max()\n   161→        if bin_edges_plot[-1] < bin_edges_plot[-2]:\n   162→            bin_edges_plot[-1] = 2*bin_edges_plot[-2] - bin_edges_plot[-3] # extrapolate to get a higher value for the last bin edge\n   163→    bin_centers = (bin_edges_plot[:-1] + bin_edges_plot[1:]) / 2\n   164→    return bin_centers\n   165→    \n   166→def compute_distance_to_reward_zone(position_curr, reward_zone_curr):\n   167→    ntimepoints = len(position_curr)\n   168→    reward_zone_range = reward_zone_dict[reward_zone_curr]\n   169→    distance_to_reward_zone = np.zeros(ntimepoints)\n   170→    idx_before_zone = position_curr < reward_zone_range[0]\n   171→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_before_zone] = position_curr[idx_before_zone] - reward_zone_range[0] # negative if before the zone\n   172→    idx_after_zone = position_curr > reward_zone_range[1]\n   173→    distance_to_reward_zone[idx_after_zone] = position_curr[idx_after_zone] - reward_zone_range[1] # positive if after the zone\n   174→    return distance_to_reward_zone\n   175→    \n   176→def max_distance_to_reward_zone = max(distance_to_reward_zone.max(), max_distance_to_reward_zone)\n   312→\n   313→        # histogram distance to reward zone into the specified bins\n   314→     \n   328→        # reward outcome\n   329→        output_curr[5] = np.any(isreward[idx]).astype(int)\n   330→        \n   331→        output.append(output_curr)\n   332→        \n   333→    if        timestamps_curr = timestamps[idx]\n   357→            for neuroni in range(nneurons):\n   358→                z = np.maximum(1,np.percentile(spike_data[spike_data[:,neuroni]>0,neuroni], 95))\n ax[1,triali].plot(timestamps_curr,input_curr[1],'.-') # vr environment\n   362→            \n   363→            reward_zone_curr = reward_zone[trial].item()\n   364→            position_curr =      ax[0,0].set_ylim(-1, max_neurons_plot+1)\n   379→        ax[0,0].set_ylabel('Neural activity')\n   380→        ax[1,0].set_ylabel('VR environment')\n   381→        ax[2,0].set_ylabel('Distance to reward zone (cm)')\n   382→        ax[2,0].legend()\n   383→        ax[3,0].set_ylabel('Position (cm)')\n   384→        ax[4,0].set_ylabel('Speed (cm/s)')\n   385→        ax[5,0].set_ylabel('Lick')\n   386→        \n   387→        plt.tight_layout()\n   388→        plt.show()\n   389→        if figfile is not None:\n   390→            fig.savefig(figfile)\n   391→        \n   392→    return neural, input, output\n   393→\n   394→def survey(force=False, datadir=DATADIR, surveyfile=SURVEYFILE):\n   395→    if not force and os.path.exists(surveyfile):\n   396→        print(f\"Loading existing survey from {surveyfile}\")\n   397→        with open(surveyfile, 'rb') as f:\n   398→            return pickle.load(f)\n   399→    all_info = {}\n   400→    subjects = get_subjects(datadir=datadir)\n   401→    all_info['reward_zone_positions'] = {}\n   402→    fig,ax = plt.subplots(1,len(subjects), figsize=(2*len(subjects),20),sharex=True,sharey=True)\n   403→\n   404→    for i,subject in enumerate(subjects):\n   405→        sessions = get_sessions(subject, datadir=datadir)\n   406→        print(f\"Subject: {subject}, Sessions: {sessions}\")\n   407→        all_info['reward_zone_positions'][subject] = np.zeros((0,2))\n   408→        for session in sessions:\n   409→            info = survey_session_data(subject, session, datadir=datadir)\n   410→            print(f\"Subject {subject}, Session {session}:\")\n   411→            for key, value in info.items():\n   412→                print(f\"  {key}: {value}\")\n   413→            for key,value in info.items():\n   414→                if key == 'reward_zone_positions':\n   415→                    all_info['reward_zone_positions'][subject] = np.concatenate((all_info['reward_zone_positions'][subject], value), axis=0)\n   416→                elif key == 'neural_time_bin_size':\n   417→                    if 'neural_time_bin_size' not in all_info:\n   418→                        all_info['neural_time_bin_size'] = [value,]\n   419→                    else:\n   420→                        all_info['neural_time_bin_size'].append(value)\n   421→                elif not ('range' in key or 'unique' in key):\n   422→                    if 'range_'+key not in all_info:\n   423→                        all_info['range_'+key] = (value, value)\n   424→                    else:\n   425→                        all_info['range_'+key] = (min(all_info.get('range_'+key, (value, value))[0], value), max(all_info.get('range_'+key, (value, value))[1], value))\n   426→                elif key not in all_info:\n   427→                    all_info[key] = value\n   428→                elif 'range' in key:\n   429→                    all_info[key] = (min(all_info[key][0], value[0]), max(all_info[key][1], value[1]))\n   430→                elif 'unique' in key:\n   431→                    all_info[key] = np.unique(np.concatenate((all_info[key], value)))\n   432→                else:\n   433→                    raise ValueError(f\"Unexpected key in info: {key}\")\n   434→        # plot a horizontal line from reward_zone_positions[0] to reward_zone_positions[1] for each trial, with y value equal to the trial number\n   435→        reward_zone_positions = all_info['reward_zone_positions'][subject]\n   436→        for trial in range(reward_zone_positions.shape[0]):\n   437→            ax[i].plot(reward_zone_positions[trial], [trial, trial], '-')\n   438→        ax[i].set_title(f\"Subject {subject}\")\n   439→\n   440→                    \n   441→    print(\"Summary across all sessions:\")\n   442→    for key, value in all_info.items():\n   443→        if key == 'reward_zone_positions':\n   444→            continue\n   445→        elif 'range' in key:\n   446→            print(f\"  {key}: {value[0]} to {value[1]}\")\n   447→        elif 'unique' in key:\n   448→            print(f\"  {key}: {value}\")\n   449→        else:\n   450→            print(f\"  {key}: {value}\")\n   451→            \n   452→    ax[0].set_xlabel('Position (cm)')\n   453→    ax[0].set_ylabel('Trial')\n   454→    fig.tight_layout()\n   455→    fig.savefig('reward_zone_positions.png')\n   456→    fig.savefig('reward_zone_positions.pdf')\n   457→    fig.savefig('reward_zone_positions.svg')\n   458→    \n   459→    segment_results = segment_reward_positions(all_info['reward_zone_positions'])\n   460→    all_info['reward_zone_labels'] = {k: v['zone_labels'] for k,v in segment_results.items()}\n   461→    all_info['reward_zone_states'] = {k: v['states'] for k,v in segment_results.items()}\n   462→    all_info['reward_zone_dist'] = {k: v['zone_dist'] for k,v in segment_results.items()}\n   463→    \n   464→    with open(surveyfile, 'wb') as f:\n   465→        pickle.dump(all_info, f)\n   466→        \n   467→    return all_info\n   468→\n   469→def segment_reward_positions(reward_zone_positions):\n   470→    # assign each trial to one of the reward zones defined in reward_zone_dict\n   471→    # use viterbi algorithm with a high cost for switching to encourage stable assignments\n   472→    zone_names = sorted(reward_zone_dict.keys())\n   473→    n_states = len(zone_names)\n   474→    zone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\n   475→    pstay = .9\n   476→    sigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\n   477→\n   478→    # transition log-probabilities\n   479→    log_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\n   480→    np.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n   481→\n   482→    results = {}\n   483→\n   484→    for mouse_id, positions in reward_zone_positions.items():\n   485→        n_trials = positions.shape[0]\n   486→        startpoints = positions[:,0] # use the start point as it looks cleaner\n   487→        valid = ~np.isnan(startpoints)\n   488→\n   489→        # emission log-probabilities: Gaussian penalty based on distance to zone range\n   490→        # distance is 0 if inside [lo, hi], otherwise distance to nearest edge\n   491→        # for NaN trials, uniform emission (all zeros) so assignment is driven by transitions\n   492→        log_emission = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states))\n   493→        dist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n   494→                          startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\n   495→        dist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\n   496→        log_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n   497→\n   498→        # viterbi forward pass\n   499→        log_delta = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states))\n   500→        psi = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states), dtype=int)\n   501→        log_delta[0] = log_emission[0]  # uniform prior\n   502→        for t in range(1, n_trials):\n   503→            for k in range(n_states):\n   504→                scores = log_delta[t - 1] + log_trans[:, k]\n   505→                psi[t, k] = np.argmax(scores)\n   506→                log_delta[t, k] = scores[psi[t, k]] + log_emission[t, k]\n   507→\n   508→        # backtrack\n   509→        states = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=int)\n   510→        states[-1] = np.argmax(log_delta[-1])\n   511→        for t in range(n_trials - 2, -1, -1):\n   512→            states[t] = psi[t + 1, states[t + 1]]\n   513→\n   514→        # convert state indices to zone names\n   515→        zone_labels = np.array([zone_names[s] for s in states])\n   516→\n   517→        # distance from each trial's startpoint to its assigned zone range\n   518→ switching to encourage stable assignments\n    zone_names = sorted(reward_zone_dict.keys())\n    n_states = len(zone_names)\n    zone_ranges = np.array([reward_zone_dict[z] for z in zone_names])  # (n_states, 2)\n    pstay = .9\n    sigma = 10.0  # std dev for Gaussian emission on zone distance\n\n    # transition log-probabilities\n    log_trans = np.full((n_states, n_states), np.log((1 - pstay) / (n_states - 1)))\n    np.fill_diagonal(log_trans, np.log(pstay))\n\n    results = {}\n\n    for mouse_id, positions in reward_zone_positions.items():\n        n_trials = positions.shape[0]\n        startpoints = positions[:,0] # use the start point as it looks cleaner\n        valid = ~np.isnan(startpoints)\n\n        # emission log-probabilities: Gaussian penalty based on distance to zone range\n        # distance is 0 if inside [lo, hi], otherwise distance to nearest edge\n        # for NaN trials, uniform emission (all zeros) so assignment is driven by transitions\n        log_emission = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states))\n        dist = np.maximum(zone_ranges[None, :, 0] - startpoints[valid, None],\n                          startpoints[valid, None] - zone_ranges[None, :, 1]) # negative if inside the zone, positive if outside\n        dist = np.maximum(dist, 0)\n        log_emission[valid] = -0.5 * (dist / sigma) ** 2\n\n        # viterbi forward pass\n        log_delta = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states))\n        psi = np.zeros((n_trials, n_states), dtype=int)\n        log_delta[0] = log_emission[0]  # uniform prior\n        for t in range(1, n_trials):\n            for k in range(n_states):\n                scores = log_delta[t - 1] + log_trans[:, k]\n                psi[t, k] = np.argmax(scores)\n                log_delta[t, k] = scores[psi[t, k]] + log_emission[t, k]\n\n        # backtrack\n        states = np.zeros(n_trials, dtype=int)\n        states[-1] = np.argmax(log_delta[-1])\n        for t in range(n_trials - 2, -1, -1):\n            states[t] = psi[t + 1, states[t + 1]]\n\n        # convert state indices to zone names\n        zone_labels = np.array([zone_names[s] for s in states])\n\n        # distance from each trial's startpoint to its assigned zone range\n        assigned_lo = zone_ranges[states, 0]\n        assigned_hi = zone_ranges[states, 1]\n        zone_dist = np.maximum(np.maximum(assigned_lo - startpoints, startpoints - assigned_hi), 0)\n        zone_dist[~valid] = np.nan\n\n        results[mouse_id] = {'zone_labels': zone_labels, 'states': states, 'zone_dist': zone_dist}\n\n    return results\n
